#6851. The Sisters Behind Dannijo Are Making Sports Merch Cool

DannijoPro was built on the idea that female fans deserve game-day gear they actually want to wear.

When Knicks frenzy overtook New York City this past summer during the team’s historic championship run, women fans—both long-suffering devotees and bandwagoners alike—went looking for something to wear. And in a city known for its fashion sense and individuality, the same old generic logo shirts and jerseys simply would not do.

DannijoPro answered the call. Seemingly overnight, the brand’s cropped blue satin bombers, bedazzled denim jackets with crochet collars, and artfully embroidered vintage sports tees took over Instagram, with celebrities like Brooke Shields, cool girls, and Ali Brunson, wife of star point guard Jalen Brunson, and Jordyn Woods, who wed center Karl-Anthony Towns shortly after he won the chip, photographed wearing DannijoPro. Knicks royalty Spike Lee even wore a DannijoPro custom jacket courtside at Game 5.

Danielle and Jodie (Danni + Jo, get it?) Snyder, the sisters and co-founders behind the bespoke fanwear company (a sporty spinoff of their original jewelry and fashion brand, Dannijo), say they were just happy to be along for the historic ride. “No one could have predicted the Knicks were going to go as far as they did, but that was amazing for us,” Jodie says of the win that ended a 53-year title drought. “People loved what we were doing.” Their Knicks finals shirts sold out three times; all 800 satin bombers (priced at $395) sold out, too. Overall, the sisters tell me their sales for 2026 are up 200 percent from last year. “I’ve never seen anything like it in my life,” says Danielle, who, along with Jodie, lived in NYC for over a decade and visited during the finals. “The energy was electric and there was such community. People were buying merch because it was so much more than a garment. It’s a piece of history. It’s a relic now.”

The Knicks run may have fueled their virality, but DannijoPro’s popularity also stems from the fact that it is serving an audience that has been ignored for too long by companies that thought that, when designing sports merch for women, it was enough to “pink it and shrink it.” And although 75 percent of women identify as fans of one or more sports, and women’s sports merch is estimated to be a $4 billion market, still a quarter of female fans say they can’t find styles they like. “It’s crazy that we’re in 2026 and you can go to a game and not find anything you want to buy as a female fan,” Jodie says.

Three months after the Knicks took home the trophy, I reach the sisters as they are gearing up for the launch of their new NBA collection for the 2026-27 season and a number of other new partnerships that have come in the wake of their viral turn, including one rolling out this month with their hometown team, the Jacksonville Jaguars—DannijoPro’s first NFL collab. Danielle is in the brand’s atelier in Tiburon in Marin County, California; as we chat on Zoom, I see people hand-stitching various garments in the background. Jodie joins the call from her car in Jacksonville, Florida, where she heads up the company’s retail and business strategy.

The sisters, who are three years apart, grew up as close as could be in Jacksonville. They’ve been creative, crafty, and entrepreneurial from a young age. Their mom, a mental health counselor, didn’t have pierced ears, but she did have a killer clip-on earring collection, and the girls were obsessed with it. In middle school, Jodie took an interest in making jewelry of her own, and Danielle, wanting to do everything her big sister did, followed right along.

Their dad is a cardiologist, and they taught themselves how to do wire work using his medical tools. Soon, they were collaborating on intricately beaded designs and selling them in local stores. “We’d see kids around school wearing our handmade designs and they didn’t even know it was us making them,” Danielle says. “What we were doing was really fresh and cool. Girls and women could take our accessories and update a white tee and jeans, or anything basic, and make it their own. That was our first taste of using style to individualize and to tell stories and we caught the bug.”

They got their big break a few years later when Danielle was in middle school. Neiman Marcus bought 150 of their turquoise nugget necklaces. “I remember staying up until 3 or 4 in the morning cranking them out,” Danielle says. “And I had an exam the next morning.” The Snyders continued to balance school and their design ambitions. Years later, when Jodie was at the University of Florida studying business and public relations and Danielle at Vanderbilt, where she majored in psychology and Spanish, Jodie would fly up and they would do trunk shows at sororities on campus. “We were always making stuff for wherever we were in life,” Jodie explains. “And everything we do is rooted in sisterhood and wanting to be together, wanting to express ourselves,” Danielle adds.

Jodie graduated from college in 2004 and moved to New York City, with Danielle following in 2007. Dannijo was founded the following year and, like the Snyders’ childhood company, quickly became known for its statement jewelry pieces. Their collection was sold in Bergdorf Goodman and Henri Bendel; characters on Gossip Girl and anchors on the Today show wore their necklaces; Beyoncé and Kim Kardashian did too (purchasing them themselves). “I remember the saleswoman calling us and saying, ‘You’re never going to believe who bought the Ruth Celia necklace,’” Danielle says. It was Queen B, who went on to wear it at Coachella, courtside at NBA games, and on a trip with her husband, Jay-Z, in Europe. “So we started getting press before we even really knew what PR was.”

They soon expanded into fashion, designing the “perfect silk slip dress” modeled after Kate Moss in the ’90s. When Jodie’s daughter was 3, she went to a tie-dye party and the next thing they knew, Dannijo was selling tie-dyed hoodies. They styled it over a slip dress and it went viral. “We sold thousands of tie-dye hoodies—it was insane,” Jodie says. “Basically, we just made things that we wanted to wear.” Collabs with Starbucks, Mercedes-Benz, and Swarovski followed.

In 2020, as the pandemic took hold in NYC, the sisters, like many residents, decamped for other parts of the country. Jodie moved back to Florida, while Danielle moved to the Bay Area. The move across the country was hard on Danielle, who was suffering from postpartum depression. “I was lost, lonely, and I didn’t have community,” she notes.

One day, her husband came home with Golden State tickets and told her they were going out. “That first Warriors game changed my life,” Danielle explains. “It changed my perspective. It got me excited again—I was excited to root for a team and to be a part of something.” They started attending games regularly and Danielle began creating her own designs to wear. “I started going through my husband’s closet and cutting up all of his old jerseys and making them into outfits for me,” she says. “My fix is always to be creative, so I was using my hands, doing stitch work, adding crystals, what have you.” Other female fans at the games began taking notice of her creative designs and asking where she had bought them. “Everyone was noticing because there was just nothing like it out there, so I called Jodie and I said, ‘This is really big.’” Soon, Dannijo’s sporty sister brand, DannijoPro, was born.

Launching a company focused on women fans was a “no-brainer,” Danielle says. “When I started making myself stuff to wear, that’s when I noticed what a crazy white space it was. Sports have never been more popular, but the product just had not caught up to that reality.” It took a year and a half to get an NBA license, but once they did, the first DannijoPro collection, which rolled out in 2024 at the Chase Center (where the Warriors play), quickly sold out.

As ever, the sisters are their own inspiration. Jodie has two children and Danielle has three. “Our worlds have changed,” Jodie says. “We’re both in T-shirts and sweatshirts; we’re not getting dressed up every day anymore.” As such, the DannijoPro items they make aren’t just for game day. They are for the 70 percent of women fans who say they’ve bought sports merch, while only 51 percent have bought tickets to attend an event. “We wanted to create items that people want to wear on and off the court or field,” Jodie says. “It's about making flattering silhouettes and clothes we all want to wear, and then applying them to the teams that we’re excited about.”

DannijoPro has three main products: officially licensed merch complete with the signature touches the designers have come to be known for, like crochet collars, hand-stitching, painting, and crystal work; “blanks” that can be customized with the help of its in-house artists (most orders come via DM); and vintage pieces that are reworked by hand.

As Jodie talks, Danielle runs around the atelier pulling out products from their new collections to show me. In August, they launched designs they made for avatars in the NBA 2K game. In September, they debuted a collab with Roger Federer’s Laver Cup tennis tournament. Their new NBA collection, called “Game Day 2.0,” rolled out last month, while their Jacksonville Jaguars line will go on sale on November 17. Future partnerships with other teams and leagues are in the works.

The sisters see their new company as “so much bigger than a fashion brand,” Danielle says. “We are reimagining fandom through a culture lens, and it’s about people. We’re living in a world of AI where everything’s like a bot. For us, it’s like, how do we bring really cool dynamic experiences in real life to people and create connections and community?”

“We think women fans should be celebrated in that there are products out there that speak to us,” Jodie adds. “It’s more than merch. I think it stands for so many other things. We can’t just accept that it’s okay to just slap logos on things, or pink it and shrink it, and women are just going to go with that. And at the end of the day, all these companies forget, but women have the buying power.”

#6850. Redesigned Microsoft Copilot Will Build Apps and Work on Its Own

Micorosft has announced that it's redesigning Copilot, the company's all-encompassing AI assistant, so users can hand off complex tasks, build tools by describing what they need, and assign work to an agent that keeps going after they log off.

The new Copilot experience has three main parts: Home, Code, and Autopilot. It also brings Word, Excel, and PowerPoint into the Copilot app. For users, the pitch is simple: Start a task in one place and stay there as it grows from a quick question into a document, an app, or a longer-running project. Most of the new capabilities are still rolling out or in preview.

Home puts Chat and Copilot Cowork together. Chat remains the place for quick answers, research, and drafts. Cowork handles work a user wants to delegate, such as preparing a briefing or pulling together a response to a request for proposals. Microsoft said Copilot eventually will route requests to Chat, Cowork, or Code on its own.

Office in Copilot will allow users to create a Word document, Excel workbook, or PowerPoint deck without leaving that experience. The files remain editable in the regular Office apps, and colleagues can work on them at the same time. Someone building a presentation, for example, could draw on a workbook, draft the slides, and keep refining both files from within Copilot.

Code is aimed at people who need a tool but might never open a development environment. A user can describe a tracker, dashboard, app, or workflow in plain language and have Copilot build it. Microsoft calls these "small, purpose-built solutions." The feature uses the same underlying technology as GitHub Copilot and runs in a sandboxed environment. A separate Copilot Managed Runtime, now in preview, lets organizations host and share the resulting apps within Microsoft 365.

Then there is Autopilot, previously known as Scout. It is designed for work that does not end with a single response: following a discussion, checking in with stakeholders, or keeping a project moving over several days. "Give it a name, a role, and a goal," Microsoft said. Autopilot runs in the cloud and has its own identity, memory, and workspace in the customer's tenant. Microsoft said its actions are subject to permissions, auditing, and organizational controls.

Those longer-running tasks also change how customers pay for Copilot. In a separate pricing post, Microsoft said everyday work in Chat and Microsoft 365 apps will remain covered by a per-user subscription. Cowork, Code, and Autopilot will be billed separately through usage-based Copilot Credits. A Copilot user subscription will still be needed to access those features.

For enterprise customers, the features that charge by usage will stay off until an admin sets up a spending policy in the Microsoft 365 admin center. Microsoft said there will be no charges before then. Admins can set budgets for the whole organization or for specific groups, limit how much individual users can spend, and get alerts when costs approach a set amount. Reports will show who is using the features and which tasks are driving costs.

Microsoft is bringing Code and Copilot Managed Runtime under those cost controls alongside Cowork. Copilot Studio agents are expected to follow in October. Before turning the features on for more employees, IT departments will need to decide who can use them, how much they can spend and which tasks are worth the extra cost.

Home and Code will begin rolling out through Microsoft's Frontier early access program in the coming weeks. Code's Frontier rollout starts at the end of September, when Autopilot also expands to private preview.

For more information, visit the Microsoft site.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

#6848. 9 Bedroom Makeover Tips for More Sex

And we’re starting with things you already have.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Your partner carries you into the bedroom à la your favorite #BookTok MMC, only for you both to remember the duvet is still covered with laundry that needs to be folded. Buzzkill. Or maybe you reach into your nightstand drawer for your wand vibrator, only to graze a stack of unpaid bills. Never mind…

“Your bedroom is already shaping how you feel about sex,” says Ashley Giddens, interior designer, artist and founder of Ashthetics NYC. It's not just clutter that can pull you out of the moment, or a tidy space that can help you stay in it, either, she says.

“Light impacts how you feel, which matters a lot when you're about to take your clothes off, while the textures impact what you feel beyond skin,” Giddens explains. Not to mention, the colors on the walls, sheets, and even the ceiling can influence how cozy, intimate, or downright sexy your bedroom feels, she says.

With that, a little bedroom makeover can help set the stage for more (and better) sex.

According to Philadelphia-based ASSECT-certified sex therapist and clinical licensed social worker Melanie Greenberg, LCSW, CST, MEd, Founder of Pulling Threads Therapy, changing your surroundings isn't a guaranteed libido booster. “But having a bedroom that is decorated and designed to make you comfortable, free of worry, relaxed, and connected to your senses and partner may make it easier to get in the mood,” she says.

Rest assured, you don't need a huge budget to bring it to life. Whether you've got $20 or $20,000 to spare, there's a way to make your bedroom a little sexier. Here are nine expert-approved places to start.

You might think a bedroom designed with sleep in mind and one designed for, well, doing it are two different beasts. But actually they are the same side of the coin: “If your bedroom doesn't help you sleep, you're probably not going to have much energy left for anything else,” Erica Corpening, co-founder and designer of Hidden Honey Homes, an adult-only boutique retreat emporium, tells Cosmopolitan.

On a physiological level, skimping on Zzz's can disrupt our endocrine system, potentially interfering with the hormones that support sexual functioning in the first place. That's why before you start shopping for silk sheets and sex swings, Corpening suggests that when designing a sex-friendly bedroom, “start with sleep.”

First and foremost, that means getting a comfortable mattress—whatever that means to you. In particular, however, take a beat to consider your preferred squishiness, how much the mattress squeaks when you move, and the weight limit. After all, if you can't get comfortable lying still, odds are you won't enjoy getting busy in missionary.

It also means considering the bed height you enjoy most. “A low platform frame can make the room feel grounded, relaxed, and a little more lounge-like,” says Corpening. It can also make getting into floor-based sex positions a whole lot easier. But it might not be accessible for people with hip or knee issues, or anyone who has difficulty getting up from low surfaces. On the flip side, a higher bed frame can give your room a more elevated, hotel-suite feel, while also making certain standing sex positions easier to pull off.

The lowest-cost suggestion on the list doubles as the most essential: Tidy up. “One of the sexiest things you can add to a bedroom is space,” says Corpening.

The debris of life (i.e., burp cloths, bills, yesterday's boxers) can keep your mind focused on everything on your to-do list—a serious turn-off (or sexual brake) for anyone whose desire is easily derailed by distraction. And don't underestimate the wonders that under-the-bed storage, vacuum-sealed storage bags, and drawer organizers—all of which are just an Amazon order or Target run away—can do for your daily detritus (and, by extension, your sex life).

“A firm, stable surface at the right angle takes the strain off knees, wrists, hips, and lower backs, so you can stay in a position long enough to actually enjoy it,” he says. In fact, if you're tempted to go full black-walled for the ~drama~, she suggests using these colors instead. “They will give you the same moody depth with some warmth still in it, unless a little dungeon energy is exactly the goal,” she says.

Whatever shade you choose, don't commit based on how it looks at high noon. Reds, pinks, and plums tend to get richer, while a cool blue or gray that looks crisp in daylight can go flat and muddy,” she says.

Yet “it's the surface you look at most when you're lying in bed, it's usually still builder white, and there's no furniture to work around, so a gallon of paint and a weekend afternoon gets you a completely different room.” Cheers to efficiency!

Of course, the color you use on the ceiling matters. “A ceiling bounces that color back down onto your skin,” she says. “My one hard rule is no green on the ceiling, because that same reflection casts green onto faces and bodies, which is about as unsexy as color gets.”

“Sheets are the biggest piece of color that sits right next to your skin, and fabric reflects color the same way a ceiling does,” she says. As such, she suggests picking them the way you'd choose a makeup shade. “Pink, peach, deep red, and plum bounce warmth back onto your face and body, while gray-green does the opposite.”

A broken mirror might mean seven years of bad luck, but a broken mirror above your bed could mean a trip to the ER. So make sure yours is professionally and securely installed.

But you have other options, too. “If you place a mirror across from a chaise, or beside a doorway, play can spill into other parts of the space,” says Corpening. “You might catch a bare shoulder in your peripheral vision, or the two of you moving together in the glass, and then it's gone. It can heighten your awareness of your body and build anticipation without anyone saying a word,” she says.

Meanwhile, a mirror near a window can serve double duty. “If you hang it near a window, it bounces daylight around and makes the room feel open during the day,” says Corpening. A win for afternoon nookie fans, indeed.

#6847. The Woven Tote Bag Trend Is Taking Over Runways and Workdays

Woven bags have officially reached icon status.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Is there any other bag that works as hard in real life as the tote bag? When it comes to being on duty and looking good, probably not. The humble tote has come in all iterations: lightweight cotton, supple leather, and suede. But more recently, the runways have been obsessed with making the oversize tote-style bags in luscious jumbo woven iterations. We saw them on the spring/summer 2027 runways at Dries Van Noten, Courrèges, Bottega Veneta, and Altuzarra. And then, labels like Bembien, Naghedi, and Dragon Diffusion are pumping out woven totes like there’s no tomorrow.

A big woven bag offers up texture and interest in a sea of ubiquitous canvas logo totes. It also gives you a bit of resilience so you don’t have to baby your bag on, say, a subway commute or a brisk morning walk where the risk of bumping into others is second nature. It is the working girl’s best friend. That’s just one of the reasons why fashion retailers have fallen head over heels for the style lately.

“Regardless of lifestyle, everyone needs a bag that carries a lot, and potentially the kitchen sink,” Ryan Kleman, Moda Operandi’s VP of buying, accessories, tells ELLE. The great news about a woven bag is that it also seamlessly goes with everything. Tone it down for a day at the office with a button-down top or a casual dress, or take it out for a nice dinner; a silk scarf tied about the strap can be the pop of color or embellishment you might crave for a more formal occasion.

In the case of some of these brands, the woven tote is literal fashion history. Bottega Veneta, for example, first introduced its signature “Intrecciato” woven leather technique in 1975. The fashion house’s spring/summer 2027 collection modernized the technique by applying it to enormous, hulking bags that swayed when worn over the shoulder. Paired with layered sweaters and matching leather belts, the bags felt like a decidedly fresh take on wearable artisanship by creative director Louise Trotter.

In the case of Dries Van Noten, one model carried an absolutely huge woven raffia-effect foldover clutch. Styled with a heather gray hoodie rolled up to reveal leather bubblegum-pink opera gloves with a biker-style cutout and buckle at the hand, the bag itself proved just how versatile a woven piece can be.

If you’re indeed thinking of upgrading your everyday work bag for a woven tote, it’s better to think bigger. “The oversized scale of the bags and the woven construction provide the perfect details to convert a chore into a fashion statement,” says Kleman. “At Moda, we have dubbed this trend the Big A** Bag and our customers are loving it.” This, too, is true: the bigger the weave, the better the carryall.

#6846. AI Leaders Warn of Risks at United Nations Security Council Briefing

The heads of OpenAI, Anthropic, and Hugging Face recently joined AI researcher Yoshua Bengio at the United Nations headquarters in New York on September 23 to warn the UN Security Council about the security risks of increasingly capable AI.

They did not offer a single plan for managing those risks. Bengio called for licensing and independent safety reviews. OpenAI's Sam Altman proposed common standards that countries could adopt through their own laws. Anthropic's Dario Amodei urged AI developers to slow down when safety work cannot keep pace. Hugging Face's Clément Delangue warned that limiting access to capable systems could leave defenders at a disadvantage.

France, which held the Security Council's rotating presidency, convened the meeting on AI and international security. The discussion exposed a difficult question for governments: how to oversee technology that crosses borders when countries disagree about who should set the rules.

Bengio, co-chair of the UN's Independent International Scientific Panel on AI, challenged the idea that competition forces companies to keep building more powerful systems. "The race is not a law of nature: it is the product of choices," he said in his published remarks. "They've told us they would slow down if they could. They can."

He called on developers to show independent experts that frontier systems are safe to train and deploy. He also proposed incident reporting, licensing, and liability insurance. Bengio identified catastrophic misuse, concentrated power, and the loss of human control as risks that require international cooperation.

Altman acknowledged the danger of moving too quickly. "We have unilaterally slowed down in the past. We will do so in the future," he said, according to remarks published by OpenAI. Decisions about AI's direction "cannot be made by labs in San Francisco alone," he added.

Altman proposed complementary national and international standards to measure frontier AI capabilities, assess risks, and test safeguards. He said countries should be able to compare evidence and verify commitments. He also called for faster reporting of serious incidents and secure channels for sharing emerging threats. Such standards should accommodate both open and closed models without favoring established companies, he said.

Amodei made a more direct commitment about the pace of Anthropic's releases: "We will slow down as much as necessary," he told the council. He identified two principal concerns: the misuse of AI to develop biological weapons and the possibility that advanced systems could outstrip developers' ability to control them.

The Anthropic chief proposed placing outside evaluators inside AI companies with access comparable to employees. He suggested that governments begin with a narrow agreement against using AI to make biological weapons, then develop ways to evaluate models and verify commitments across borders. He also called for common testing standards and a system for notifying other countries of significant AI incidents.

Delangue drew on a July incident in which Hugging Face disclosed an AI agent cyberattack. He called for stronger monitoring and disclosure, but argued that safety also depends on giving defenders access to capable tools. The central danger, he said, was "asymmetry of powerful AI" between attackers and defenders, large companies and everyone else, and wealthy countries and the rest of the world. His team used an open-source model while responding to the attack, he said.

The U.S. government opposed a broader international regulatory role. White House science adviser Michael Kratsios told the council that countries should develop their own ability to use and govern the technology. In the U.S. statement, he rejected new global governance structures and used the phrase "superintelligence, formerly known as AI," adopting President Donald Trump's terminology from his General Assembly speech the previous day.

The four AI briefers differed on how far oversight should go and how widely capable systems should be available. They agreed on a narrower point: Governments need better ways to understand what advanced AI systems can do and to detect when safeguards fail.

John K. Waters is the editor in chief of a number of Converge360.com sites, with a focus on high-end development, AI and future tech. He's been writing about cutting-edge technologies and culture of Silicon Valley for more than two decades, and he's written more than a dozen books. He also co-scripted the documentary film Silicon Valley: A 100 Year Renaissance, which aired on PBS.  He can be reached at [email protected].

In its latest threat intelligence report, Ontinue found that attackers are increasingly using trusted workplace tools to trick users into giving them access to enterprise networks.

Cybercriminals are moving away from mass phishing scams and focusing on more targeted attacks, often using trusted websites and services to trick victims, according to a new report from KnowBe4.

Anthropic announced it is introducing machine-readable watermarks to text generated by supported Claude models as it moves to comply with European Union AI transparency rules.

OpenAI has introduced Dots, GPT-6 Astra-powered agents that run on their own cloud computers, using connected applications to carry on with assignments between conversations and incorporating users' feedback as they go.

#6845. Misha & Puff’s First Womenswear Collection Gives Knitwear a New Point of View

The cult-favorite children’s knitwear brand is officially expanding to adults.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Women’s fashion has some of the best designs out there—full stop. That said, every now and then I see a well-dressed kid and think, “Damn, I wish I had something like that.” This is especially true when I see a little one sporting a design from none other than Misha & Puff, a brand known for its ethically crafted, handmade, vintage-inspired children’s clothes. And while my wardrobe carries a playful touch, I’m not necessarily looking for a supersized version of a child’s sweater, but rather the essence of that whimsy. Thankfully, I don’t have to look too far; Misha & Puff just launched its first-ever women’s collection—and it’s more than I could have hoped for.

“Knits are hot. Knits are sexy. The way that they drape, the way that they accentuate your neck, I really do feel like we can do a lot with a knit,” Sophia Roe told ELLE.com over the phone. “For me, I feel really beautiful when I put these clothes on.” The James Beard Award-winning chef—along with her daughter, Mizu—came together with artist Mara Hoffman behind the lens to create a campaign that captures the essence of Misha & Puff, highlighting the craftsmanship and expression of the brand’s new womenswear collection.

“Everything I do with the brand is very authentic and really about what I’m interested in...That’s how the kids’ brand started, with me having my son and then growing up,” Misha & Puff’s founder, Anna Wallack, said on a phone call. “The choice to launch women’s in a bigger way really comes from the pull that I’m feeling as a woman. For me, dressing women and dressing myself is about how we’re working these pieces together. How do I feel in these silhouettes? Who is this character today? I want to keep playing with that in a bigger way.”

Wallack went on to tell me that designing for the collection—which launched today—started with what she described as a “fiber-first” approach. “We work with a Pima cotton in Peru that worked so well for kids because it’s incredibly soft and it’s so wearable, and then to find that I want to wear that all the time for myself; I want to wear our merino wool,” she explained. “Our decision to launch women’s with a bigger collection really was a commitment to doing it instead of just making one sweater here and there.” The result? A range of expressive knits in a color palette reminiscent of the ’70s that encourage the wearer to be who she is as a woman today. With every solid- or heather-hued piece there’s another featuring an eye-catching print or embroidery detail. “I feel a little more permission to explore other ideas in the adult collection and what it means to be a women, too.”

This exploration extends into Misha & Puff’s campaign. Roe shared on a phone call that the idea of partnering with Misha & Puff stemmed from just being a fan of the brand. “I said, ‘I don’t know what we can do but I really want to do something with Anna.’ Little did I know that in the background she was working on this unbelievable adult collection,” she said. Though this isn’t the first project Roe has starred in with her daughter, she explained it was the first where she was shot by another mother, making the experience feel truly special—especially as she comes into her body 15 months postpartum. “I feel kind of hot as a mom. There are a lot of narratives out there about getting back to whatever that pre-baby thing is, and I just kind of rebuke that in every single way. I think what I am now is kind of amazing and I really do feel like Mara was able to bring that out in a way that no one else could have.”

On the vision for the campaign, Hoffman shared that the focus was “seeing how does a woman exist through a space or on the street, and how does she live in it?” Working to capture Roe in a documentarian way, the artist wanted to make sure the imagery felt authentic to both the brand and the people wearing it. “We are lucky to be able to capture little shots of that along the way.” Of course, shooting with a baby can come with its own unpredictability. Luckily, as Roe shared, working with someone like Hoffman helped her relax on set with the reminder, “This is just life.”

#6839. This Just In: People Are Collecting Cum

Time to get into the details on the cumdumping kink.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Rawdoggers across the globe have long waxed poetic about the intimacy-boosting, oh-so-connective appeal of sex without condoms, despite the potential risks (unwanted pregnancy, STIs… the list goes on). Still, if one load feels good, does that mean multiple feel even better? Well, that's the idea behind cumdumping.

Cumdumping is an emerging kink in which an individual has condomless sex with multiple people during a single encounter, with the explicit goal of taking as many loads as possible (a metric known within the community as load count), COSRT-accredited psychosexual and relationship psychotherapist Silva Neves, author of articles on cumdumping, tells Cosmopolitan.

The term itself isn’t necessarily new and has long been used as an insult hurled against those seen as sexually non-discerning and promiscuous. In recent years, however, “it’s been reclaimed in the bareback gay men's culture to be associated with the giving of pleasure,” says Neves. Though that sentiment is not universal: “In the heterosexual world the word is still an insult, particularly when attributed to women,” he notes.

Still, its surge among sexual subcultures has caught the attention of sexuality researchers. In a July 2026 essay published in Sexual and Relationship Therapy, Neves explored the practice through conversations with 11 queer men who engage in cumdumping. The findings suggest that, sure, part of the appeal lies not only in the collection. But that thwarting taboos, feeling desirable, and building community fuel cumdumpers, too. A closer look ahead.

In the context of queer men, a cumdump specifically refers to a man who allows and encourages multiple men to top them bareback, ultimately ejaculating into their anus, says Neves. Other sexual communities haven't embraced the term in quite the same way, but among women and people of other genders, it may also refer to receiving semen vaginally or orally.

What sets cumdumping apart from other multiple-partnered play is that the goal is usually to bang then bounce. “The quicker the better because the goal is the ‘load count’ rather than a long intercourse session with one person,” Neves tells Cosmopolitan. While group sex might involve extended sexual encounters with multiple people, cumdump events typically prioritize the number of ejaculations over the length of any individual interaction. Once a participant finishes, they're generally on their merry way.

The number of loads, and therefore number of people, is usually pretty damn high, with some cumdumping events having reportedly seen recipients take more than a hundred loads. But few orgies ever reach double digits, let alone triple digits.

Just as different pleasure-seekers enjoy spit and praise for different reasons, “different people might be drawn to this fantasy for different reasons, so there’s unlikely to be a singular or universal explanation,” Justin Lehmiller, PhD, a social psychologist, research fellow at The Kinsey Institute, and author of Tell Me What You Want, tells Cosmopolitan. Still, emerging research pulls out these main themes.

If you've ever swiped a finger through the frosting of a cake you were explicitly told not to taste, you know being told you shouldn't do something can make you want to do it even more. Turns out, the same psychology can carry over into our bed chambers, too.

“When you find the idea of something even slightly appealing, and then you’re told you can’t or shouldn’t do it, that tends to increase the desire/motivation to explore it,” explains Lehmiller. “When something becomes taboo, that adds an erotic charge to it because we have to break a rule or take on some degree of risk actually to do it.”

Given the taboo surrounding condomless sex with multiple partners, it's not hard to see how cumdumping could scratch that particular erotic itch.

For some queer men, the appeal goes beyond the thrill of breaking the rules. It's about reclaiming the very sexuality that has long been stigmatized and policed.

Sex between men has long been treated as taboo. And, even with the advent of PrEP and effective HIV treatment, bareback (condomless) sex between men remains steeped in stigma left over from the AIDS epidemic, Neves tells Cosmopolitan. Cumdumping and otherwise “having condom-free sex with multiple strangers is often judged as ‘reckless’ behavior or even pathologized as ‘self-harm’,” he says.

Advances in HIV prevention have also made it possible for more people to explore fantasies that once felt too risky to act on. “Before PrEP, cumdump was a popular fantasy, but few practiced it,” Neves says. So, nowadays, for some queer men, cumdumping can feel like a celebration of queer sexuality on their own terms. “Many cumdumps I spoke to mentioned sexual freedom as one of the main arousing elements of it,” he says.

Of course, queer men aren't the only ones whose sex lives have been policed. Queer and heterosexual women and people of other genders have faced their own forms of sexual stigma, including purity politics and slut-shaming. For some, having sex with multiple people at once—in a cumdump event or otherwise—can feel like taking a red marker to those tired sexual scripts and reclaiming their sexuality on their own terms.

...and again and again and again. In Neves's 2026 research, participants shared that another part of the appeal was the validation of being desired by multiple men, each of whom chose them as the recipient of their grand finale.

“When men ejaculate, there is a refractory period, which means that the sexual activity is over for them,” explains Neves. “Some cumdumps feel very aroused at knowing tops chose them to conclude their sexual act, or to relieve their horniness,” he says.

The advent of X, Grindr, and other social and hook-up platforms makes it easier than ever for cumdump enthusiasts to find one another. And, in countries where PrEP is accessible, Neves reports that communities are emerging around the practice.

Some cumdumps even affectionately refer to themselves as “community holes,” a term that reflects their desire to be sexually available to other men within the queer community. And Neves found that both cumdumpees and cumdumpers exchange tips, and sometimes organize events together. For queer folks historically excluded from traditional spaces of belonging, that sense of community can be especially meaningful.

Even with our abysmally lacking sex education, it likely won’t surprise you to learn that “the primary medical considerations [of cumdumping] are exposure to sexually transmitted infections (STIs),” according to board-certified physician Dr. Stacy De-Lin, MD, the associate medical director of Planned Parenthood Hudson Peconic in Hudson, New York.

Each additional partner adds another potential source of exposure, De Lin says. “The risk isn’t necessarily about the quantity of semen itself, but about factors such as the STI status of each partner, the type of sexual contact, and the prevention strategies being used,” she says.

Anal intercourse generally carries a higher STI risk compared to other sexual activities, given that without proper warm-up and lubrication, repeated penetration may also cause microtrauma to the delicate rectal tissues, potentially increasing susceptibility to certain infections, she explains. Class CrunchLabs was launched last year to offer free hands-on science curriculum resources including teaching guides, classroom slides, student readings and handouts, assessments, and instructional videos featuring Rober alongside "Science Bob" Pflugfelder, celebrity guests, and student co-hosts. The initiative was backed by a $7.2 million grant to create the CrunchLabs.org Foundation, a nonprofit "dedicated to revolutionizing science education in a way that promotes critical thinking and inspires lasting curiosity and confidence in STEM," according to a news announcement at the time.

The organization plans to roll out additional courses on YouTube over time, with the ultimate goal of posting more than 1,000 science videos in 34 languages.

Like all nail polish colors, green has a vast spectrum, one that runs from electric neon to bold kelly to soft sage and all the way down to deep, moody olive. Of all the greens out there, olive sits in a quieter place. The hue is earthy and a little mysterious, somewhere between a vintage army jacket and an actual juicy olive. It’s a shade we love for fall, since it pairs so seamlessly with black, rich browns, burgundy, red, and, of course, cream.

Once reserved for the brave few, olive has now gone mainstream. Part of the appeal is how wearable it is, offering an unexpected twist without ever feeling too loud. It’s also soft and soothing to the eye, making it an easy shade to wear day after day.

If you’re new to the shade, the easiest way in is to treat it like a neutral. Try it with a camel coat and chunky knits when the weather turns, or a crisp white shirt for something fresh and unexpected. A creamy, opaque finish feels polished and modern, while a glossy jelly or a hint of shimmer adds depth in low light. However you wear it, olive looks effortlessly considered and adds an edge to your style.

#6833. OpenAI Getting Rid of Custom GPTs and Moving to Plugin Architecture

OpenAI has announced it will retire Custom GPTs and move the specialized workflows built around them to a new plugin architecture spanning ChatGPT and its Codex coding agent.

Custom GPT retirement is currently scheduled for Dec. 11, 2026, although the company says timing and migration availability can vary by account and workspace.

For affected Enterprise workspaces, OpenAI's current migration schedule targets Oct. 26 for ending creation of new Custom GPTs. Qualifying Enterprise customers with approved deferrals can continue using them until Feb. 11, 2027.

OpenAI has changed its UI to make GPTs less prominent, and when users choose to try creating a new Custom GPT, they are now prompted with an option to create a plugin instead.

Rather than maintaining separate customized versions of ChatGPT, OpenAI is shifting customization toward plugins that can package reusable instructions, reference material, and connections to outside services.

Under OpenAI's plugin architecture, a plugin can include one or more skills containing instructions and resources for repeatable workflows, a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server exposing tools and external systems, or both. ChatGPT and Codex share a universal plugin directory, although some capabilities can remain specific to a particular environment.

For organizations, that makes the change more than a simple renaming exercise. A company could package recurring workflows into skills while using MCP servers or connected apps to give those workflows access to enterprise systems and data.

OpenAI says creators will be able to migrate an existing GPT through the My GPTs interface. The GPT's instructions become a skill in the replacement plugin, while knowledge files are copied into plugin reference files and connected apps can be carried over.

Not everything transfers automatically. Existing GPT conversations do not migrate, the selected model does not carry over, and Custom Actions may have to be rebuilt using an available app or a custom MCP server.

Sharing also changes. Migrated plugins start private, existing GPT users do not automatically receive access to the replacement, and prior sharing settings do not carry over.

OpenAI is also cautioning organizations against assuming that migration will preserve behavior exactly. "A migrated plugin may respond differently," the company says, recommending that users retest familiar prompts, reference-material use, required output formats, and integrations before relying on the replacement.

That warning has contributed to pushback from some GPT builders, who argue that plugins do not fully reproduce the dedicated and predictable environment of a Custom GPT. Other complaints have focused on sharing, migration complexity, and differences in how migrated workflows use instructions and reference material.

Despite those concerns, OpenAI is clearly consolidating around plugins as its common mechanism for extending its AI products. Its developer documentation now describes plugins as packages users can "discover, install, share, and publish" across ChatGPT and Codex, replacing the separate-custom-assistant model with reusable AI capabilities that can be combined with enterprise tools and services.

For more information, go to the OpenAI site.

David Ramel is an editor and writer at Converge 360.

In its latest threat intelligence report, Ontinue found that attackers are increasingly using trusted workplace tools to trick users into giving them access to enterprise networks.

Cybercriminals are moving away from mass phishing scams and focusing on more targeted attacks, often using trusted websites and services to trick victims, according to a new report from KnowBe4.

Anthropic announced it is introducing machine-readable watermarks to text generated by supported Claude models as it moves to comply with European Union AI transparency rules.

OpenAI has introduced Dots, GPT-6 Astra-powered agents that run on their own cloud computers, using connected applications to carry on with assignments between conversations and incorporating users' feedback as they go.

#6832. You Can Definitely Do These Easy Nail Art Designs at Home

Take it from a fellow amateur manicurist.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Ah, the at-home manicure. Nail artists make it look so easy when you’re in the salon, but get in front of a file and dotting tool at home? Suddenly, things can get a little shaky. I’ve technically been doing my own nails since I was about six, but really started leaning into trying to get better at painting on little designs over the past few years. I can say from experience: There is a lot of so-called easy nail art out there that, in reality, is very difficult to do yourself. But there are also plenty of designs you can totally pull off as a beginner. That, my friends, is what we’re here for today.

Unfortunately, the old “practice makes perfect” thing does actually apply to any type of creative endeavor, manicures included. I’m not saying you’re going to be able to perfectly recreate any of these looks the first time you try (though, if you can, maybe quit your day job). There’s a reason I still pay a professional to do my nails if I want them to look extra super good for a special occasion; those people have years of training I’ll never be able to catch up to. Instead, these are designs with elements I know a beginner can do well enough that you won’t get so frustrated with the results that you give up on your new hobby for good.

“It can be super intimidating to start painting your nails while you're looking at an inspo pic or video from IG or Pinterest, but remember that the reason you probably started painting your nails was to have a creative outlet and a moment of self expression,” nail artist and wise person Sigourney Nuñez says. “Try to not let perfectionism get in the way of something that is meant to be a self care moment where you get to show up for yourself in this fun, creative and colorful way.”

Hear, hear! If you’re ready to get started on your artistic ungual journey, keep scrolling for expert-recommended tips, tools, and plenty of design inspiration.

While your toolkit can grow exponentially the further you get into your nail art journey, Nuñez says the “perfect DIY tool trifecta” is a clean up brush, dotting tool, and liner brush.

I can confirm the clean up brush will be your most-used tool when you’re learning to do your own nails. Even if you don’t make a massive, colored-outside-the-lines mess, going around your nail with a clean up brush dipped in nail polish remover “can really give you that crispy salon finish at home,” Nuñez says. She uses a Simply Simmons #4 painter’s brush you can buy at Michael’s, but says you could also sacrifice an old angled eyeliner brush if you aren’t looking to spend money on tools yet. “Just be sure you don’t use it on your eyes again after you’ve dunked it in nail polish remover,” she warns.

Nuñez calls dotting tools “the gateway to the nail art universe.” You could use bobby pins, a flat eraser on the end of a pencil, or the end of a brush, but there are plenty of affordable dotting tool options too. Olive & June makes a nice double-sided one that would be a great first dotting tool, while Halo Taco has a fun, holographic set that comes with a total of eight different sized tips.

Once you’re really ready to level up, get yourself a nail brush. There are sets with multiple brush sizes out there, but Nuñez doesn’t think you need them. “I literally only use one type of liner brush, [the] Liner 3 from Bio Seaweed Gel,” she says. “It’s been my go-to brush for years.” Instead of dealing with a bunch of different size brushes, you can make different size lines by varying the amount of pressure you use.

I cannot emphasize this enough: If you’re serious about wanting your manicure to look as close to professionally done as possible, you absolutely must prep your nails before you even think about picking up your base coat. This means completely removing all your polish, cutting and/or shaping your nails with a file, and cleaning up your cuticle area... but not too much on that last part.

“When it comes to DIY prep, less is more,” Nuñez says. “There is no need to over trim any surrounding skin unless there's a hangnail that's waving hi at you.” Instead, she recommends using a cuticle buffer stick like the Sally Hansen Push & Buff 2-in-1 Cuticle Pen. “It helps exfoliate the cuticle area and side walls, plus helps buff out the waxy layer that can accumulate [on your nails],” Nuñez says. “That waxy layer is totally normal but it gets in the way of adhesion.” I like to wash my hands after I’ve done all that trimming, filing, and buffing, to make sure there’s no leftover nail dust that could ruin my hard work.

Keeping your cuticles healthy between manis will make this prep step easier. “I’m actually so passionate about cuticle care,” Brooklyn-based nail artist Anna Miles says. “A little attention every day will do wonders—more than any nail tech could accomplish in our monthly sessions.” She recommends using a towel to gentle scrub your cuticle area after a shower (”to encourage the cuticle skin to roll back”) and be diligent about using hand cream and cuticle oil after you wash your hands.

“If you’re like me and out of sight equals out of mind, buy travel sizes in multiples and keep one everywhere—bag, coffee table, bathroom, bedside,” Miles says. She loves the CND Rescue Rxx Daily Keratin Treatment and Zoya Healing Dry Skin Hand & Body Cream, both of which come in smaller sizes.

The number one thing that will make your nail art look better is to learn to leverage your fulcrum finger (aka the digit that’s holding your hand steady while you work; Nuñez says this is usually the pinky) and elbows. Do not try to do your nails while sitting on a couch; you need a table with enough space to create those stabilizing points.

Whether you’re going to use a dotting tool, liner brush, sponge, or other nail art tool, it can take some practice to figure out how much polish to put on it before you start designing. I always do a test run on a piece of paper; it’s not a perfect substitution for painting on your curved nail, but I think it gives you a pretty good idea of how much polish to use. Nuñez also has a handy video on how to load your liner brush properly.

And, again, you just need to practice. When I’m in the habit of doing my nails every week, I’m shocked at how much better I get over the course of a month. Ready to dive in? Here’s some inspo to get you started.

Let’s start with the easiest nail art of them all, a skittles manicure. This is simply painting each nail with a different shade of polish, which is a fun way to start playing around with color theory—a key skill for professional-looking DIY manis. “Every great manicure starts with a beautiful color story,” Nuñez says. “When the colors can do most of the talking, you don't have to try as hard with your DIY design.”

Glitter polish is kind of a nail art hack in its own right, because the texture makes any mistakes kinda blend in. For this look, I swiped some random splotches of purple cream over a glitter from Mooncat (a brand that makes some of the best sparkles, IMO).

The half and half manicure is a great way to practice creating straight lines: Since you fill in the space on one side of the line, it’s less obvious if things are little crooked.

“When painting lines, instead of moving the brush, you can try rotating your hand/nail while keeping the brush anchored,” Nuñez advises. “This can help with a more precise application.”

I know, I know, all these straight lines look intimidating, but, similar to the half and half, I promise that the fact that you fill in the triangles after you paint on the lines makes it far easier to get a crisp look. And if you have the patience, you could always wait for the base color to try, then use Scotch tape to stencil out the details.

Marble looks complicated, but Miles swears it’s not so difficult. “Marble is good for beginners because the success of this design is in the imperfection,” she is. “Uneven, shaky lines and blurry, smeared areas... sounds like the makings of a gorgeous marble!” Pick two colors of polish with high contrast to make the marble design stand out.

Miles says there are many ways to do marble, but one of the first methods she fell in love with is using an unused kitchen sponge that’s been cut into thin slices. Start by applying one of your polishes as a base coat, then paint the other color onto a nail-sized section of your sponge slice. “You can see the marble ‘veins’ on the sponge as you paint it,” Miles says. Stamp the wet paint onto your nail, using a rocking motion so it covers the whole nail. You can add metallic accents or a shimmery top coat to really make it special.

This is one of my go-to designs. You paint on two coats on your base color, then go in with a second color but stop before you get too close to your cuticle. It’s extremely simple, but gets loads of compliments.

If you crave more intricate designs but don’t have the brush skillset just yet, stickers are a great option. “I love using nail art stickers,” Miles says, noting that her favorites come from Deco Beauty. “For best results, make sure to apply them to a long-dried base color and paint two thin top coats instead of one thick one if you’re using traditional polish.”

Polka dot nails stay trendy, so that dotting tool is definitely worth the investment. There are many different ways to do dots—a bunch of super tiny dots, big circles, a mix and match of multiple size. I’d recommend practicing on a piece of paper first so you can get the pressure just right for the size you’re looking for. A light touch is key once you’re actually on the nail. “If your base color is not fully dry and you put too much force on the dotting tool that can create a dent and hole in your hard work,” Nuñez says.

This is a design that definitely requires Nuñez’s recommended stabilization techniques, but if things get a little shaky, you can just say you were going for a Bambi effect. Larger dots can also be a bit more forgiving.

Another trick for adding details when you don’t have a super steady hand is to apply the color you want for the detail as the base coat and then layer thick strokes of another color on top. That’s what I did for this look: The blue polish went down first, then I just painted one strip of green vertically and one strip horizontally to create the rectangle accent effect.

As everything gets digitized, I fear we’ll lose important ancient techniques, like using paper hole protectors as a stencil for a negative space half moon effect. At least, that’s how I’d pull off this look.

In the vein of nail stickers, confetti polish is a great way to create nail art without even looking at an extra tool. (KB Shimmer makes really fun ones that come in a bunch of shapes and colors.) Just make sure to apply two thin coats of top coat so the sparkles stay put.

I would never in a million years suggest that a French manicure is easy. But I will tell you that those rounded silicone stamper tools absolutely work to create a micro French, especially on shorter nails.

Using a rhinestone application tool can be a bit tricky to master, but I do find it a relatively easy way to add just a touch of detail to your nails. These won’t stick with just some good top coat; you’ll need to use nail glue too.

Great news: Once you’ve gotten good at polka dots, you can totally do flowers too. These are made up of just five black dots and one white one, but the final look seems much more complicated.

Kara McGrath is a contributing beauty editor at Cosmo with over 15 years of experience writing professionally about nails. She’s been an amateur nail artist for over 30.

#6827. Zimmermann Hosts a Star-Studded Soirée in Downtown New York City

Katie Holmes, Uma Thurman, and Naomi Watts came out to celebrate the brand’s cruise 2027 collection.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Inside Indochine’s red-lit, retro-inspired French-Vietnamese dining room in New York City, Zimmermann gathered friends of the brand to celebrate its cruise 2027 collection. Presented on the sun-drenched Côte d’Azur in June, the label’s latest served as the perfect bit of vacation and occasion-ready dressing for November in New York. Now that the lineup has officially hit stores, fashion insiders and fans of the brand alike (including Uma Thurman, Eve Hewson, Katie Holmes, and Naomi Watts) showed up dripping in ruffled gowns, sailor-inspired stripes, chunky shearlings, and ethereal lace and silks from the collection. Against the dark, 1940s-inspired tropical salon interior of Indochine, it was the ideal mix of downtown energy and New York glamour.

As champagne flowed through the bar area, glasses clinked while miniature crab cakes and spring rolls were passed. Zimmermann’s sister duo Nicky and Simone Zimmermann could be spotted catching up with friends new and old. “We have a very, very close connection with New York,” Nicky told ELLE in between greeting guests. “It was pretty much when I started the business, one of the first places internationally that I went to in the ’90s, and it blew my mind. It’s been inspirational to us 100 percent.” It was a rare occasion for the brand; for years, Zimmermann has shown at Paris Fashion Week (after presenting in New York in the mid-2010s) and the duo oscillates between Sydney and Paris for most of the year.

Zimmermann’s painterly and intrinsically bohemian flourishes could be seen in the many outfits of guests, all highlighting the vivid cruise collection. Think: decadent silky draped drop-waisted gowns and paneled lace maxi dresses, or, for the more demure, sleek all-black looks that captured the New York essence. “For me, there’s a little bit of a sense of humor behind it—without insulting anybody—it was about the story when Australia actually beat America in the America’s Cup, which is a very famous sailing race,” Nicky told ELLE of the collection’s inspiration.

“I think it was in 1983, and America had not lost the America’s Cup in something like 173 years; it was a ridiculous winning streak, and Australia came along. The inspiration is more about a David and Goliath kind of story,” she continued. “We came along as the Aussies, not established in the sailing world. They were very cheeky, rogue, kind of Aussie young guys up against this amazing, sophisticated establishment of the New York Yacht Club. And they never lost—and we beat them. So in Australia, it’s a legendary story.” Nicky combined the sportiness of sailing gear with the sort of elite New York Yacht Club preppiness, resulting in colorful silk scarf embellishment or sweaters decked out in accessorized pins. “They would hold the most incredible parties and the women were so glamorous and sophisticated and beautiful and not anything that Australia could relate to at the time, so I loved the contrast.”

Conversation was flowing and blooming anthuriums covered the tables as guests took their seats. Nicky kicked off the dinner with a speech, musing on her early days in New York on the Lower East Side and the many parties she attended in Tribeca before sharing more inspiration behind the collection: “It’s about establishment, and then it’s about people, you know, working really hard, coming together as a team, defeating the undefeatable,” she said, with a laugh.

Next came an intimate performance by the singer and Bad Monkey actress Charlotte Lawrence, who sang two of her original songs, then led a folksy sing-along to “Dreams” by Fleetwood Mac. Even the most guarded of celebs and fashion’s elite couldn’t help but bob their heads and sing a tune. As guests dined on a family-style mix of kale salad, spicy shrimp, grilled salmon, barbecued chicken, and steak, the champagne glass clinks slowed as candles slowly flickered and the evening closed out with lemon tarts.

A little yacht-club polish, a little Aussie irreverence: Zimmermann celebrated cruise 2027 in high style.

#6825. AI Ambitions Are Running Into a Network Infrastructure Trust Problem

Enterprise ambitions around AI are running into a fundamental problem: Orgnanizations aren't always confident in the networks those projects depend on, according to a report from telecommunications provider Arelion.

The research found that concerns about network providers have caused organizations to rethink strategic initiatives over the past two years. Among respondents whose initiatives were affected, AI and data-driven projects were the most cited, at 49%. That put AI ahead of security and compliance programs at 44%, digital transformation at 42%, and cloud migration at 38%.

The findings come from Arelion's report, "The Trust Illusion: Why AI and Digital Transformation Need More Than a Reliable Network," based on a survey of 518 senior enterprise network decision-makers in the U.S., U.K., Germany, and France conducted by Savanta.

All respondents worked for organizations with more than 2,000 employees and influenced network strategy across data centers, cloud, and connectivity. Of those surveyed, 66% had final sign-off responsibility. The results suggest that as organizations pursue increasingly data-intensive AI projects, confidence in the network infrastructure supporting them could become another deployment obstacle.

On the surface, Arelion found little evidence of a trust crisis. A total of 93% of respondents said they largely or completely trust their current network provider, while 83% said that trust has strengthened over time.

The picture changes when respondents are asked about a serious network incident. Only 15% said they have complete confidence in their provider's ability to quickly detect and address such a problem. Another 42% said their provider seriously lets them down anywhere from a few times a year to monthly or more frequently.

Those concerns have consequences beyond the network itself. Responding organizations have delayed, scaled back, or stopped strategic initiatives because of provider trust concerns, while others have added resiliency or risk-mitigation measures.

AI and data projects were the most affected among respondents reporting an impact, as organizations try to move AI from experimentation into larger production deployments.

A serious provider failure could also have substantial consequences. Forty percent of respondents said such an event would cause major operational disruption, while 18% anticipated significant financial loss or severe financial or reputational damage.

Another tension runs through Arelion's findings. Enterprises need networks they can trust to support AI projects, but many of the same decision-makers are concerned about AI's growing role inside those networks. A total of 54% of respondents identified AI and automation in network management and security as the factors most likely to negatively affect network trust over the next three years. That ranked ahead of data sovereignty expectations and geopolitical instability, both at 39%, and regulatory requirements and quantum computing threats to current encryption standards, both at 38%.

At the same time, respondents aren't rejecting AI as a network technology. Proactive AI-driven fault prediction was among the capabilities they said would matter most to future trust, alongside stronger built-in security, greater accountability, and responsive support. That leaves enterprises in an unusual position: AI is increasing their dependence on digital infrastructure while also raising questions about how that infrastructure itself is managed.

Arelion has an interest in emphasizing the importance of network-provider trust, and the survey reflects the views specifically of network decision-makers at large enterprises. Still, the findings point to an infrastructure consideration that can get lost amid the rush to adopt new AI models and agents. AI projects ultimately depend on the networks, cloud environments, data centers, and connectivity beneath them. If organizations lack confidence in that foundation, access to better AI models alone won't remove the obstacle.

"Trust is influencing which strategic initiatives move forward, how organizations manage risk, and which providers they choose to work with," said Mattias Fridström, Vice President at Arelion. "As businesses become increasingly reliant on digital infrastructure, enterprises are looking for network providers that can demonstrate transparency, accountability, and operational excellence, not just availability."

The full report is available for download here at the Arelion site (registration required)

In its latest threat intelligence report, Ontinue found that attackers are increasingly using trusted workplace tools to trick users into giving them access to enterprise networks.

Cybercriminals are moving away from mass phishing scams and focusing on more targeted attacks, often using trusted websites and services to trick victims, according to a new report from KnowBe4.

Anthropic announced it is introducing machine-readable watermarks to text generated by supported Claude models as it moves to comply with European Union AI transparency rules.

OpenAI has introduced Dots, GPT-6 Astra-powered agents that run on their own cloud computers, using connected applications to carry on with assignments between conversations and incorporating users' feedback as they go.

#6820. Mark Ballas Responds to Speculation He Might Become a Judge on ‘Dancing With the Stars’

Mark Ballas is one of the most talented pros on Dancing With the Stars, but fans have been speculating that he could pivot to being a judge on the series after getting a taste of judging life over on Dancing With the Stars: The Next Pro.

So...does Mark plan to hang up his dancing shoes and join the judges’ panel? He told The Hollywood Reporter in a recent interview that the verdict is still out. “I don’t know. I’m not sure. I’d have to see,” he said, somewhat cryptically. “I’m a very take it day-by-day person. Honestly, I don’t think ahead like that. I’d have to see if it came along. I enjoyed guest judging. The Next Pro was really fun. I think I’ve done the show for long enough that I could give good feedback and advice for contestants. But, again, that’s totally out of my hands.”

He also noted elsewhere in the interview that “I’ve only guest judged once. I love the creative part of the show. That’s what makes it fun. That’s kind of been my life for so long. And one of the big reasons for me going back last season was excitement for my son to see me do what I’ve dedicated my life to. He’s only three, but he can watch the show and he’s like, ‘Oh my God, dada!’ So it’s a big part of it, and constantly loving the new challenges and creativity. It’s pretty straightforward, simple as that, really.”

Oh, AND? Mark was also asked about his mom, Shirley Ballas, potentially joining the Dancing With the Stars judges’ panel full-time, and teased “I have no idea. She’s already a judge on Strictly. That would be difficult to do both, but Len [Goodman] did it for so long. So did Bruno [Tonioli]. I have no idea what the intentions are with the panel. I think the panel is pretty set.”

#6817. A New Marni x Hoka Sneaker Is Here—Here’s What to Know

The Italian fashion house once again brings its unique touch to the athletic brand.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Last year, when Marni and Hoka launched their first collaboration, the two labels came together to bring the world a collection of puffy, chunky, candy-colored takes on the athletic footwear brand’s Bondi sneaker. Naturally, it was a hit among the streetwear set, including the likes of Steph Curry. Of course, a lot can change in the span of a year and a half—and the latest sneaker trends are no exception. In the same way jeans have gone from baggy styles to straight-leg and even skinny jeans, fashion has taken its sneakers from their dad eras to silhouettes that lean a little sleeker. The latest result of these turning tides: The Hoka Clifton Marni.

“Shifting from the maximalist and oversized aesthetic towards a calmer, more essential form made sense with Meryll [Rogge]’s ascension to Marni,” Thomas Cykana, Hoka’s senior director of global collaborations, tells ELLE in an email. “This affords a simple but surprising design that cues premium embellishments, metallic hardware, and unexpected prints over an overtly in-your-face design. This shift enables consumers to both craft their uniform and express their individuality without sacrificing Hoka’s pure performance capabilities.”

Available now, the design is the culmination of form and function. Marni brings its unique design perspective to the technical shoe, not only with the chosen colors—Orange, Black, and White—but also with design-forward touches such as woven heel tabs, windowpane-patterned insoles, and three shoelace options for each colorway.

“Marni has a unique ability to take everyday design cues and make them feel artful and unexpected. That lust for curiosity and imaginative nature is what first drew our team to the label and continues to inspire us,” Cykana shares. “Our debut collection in 2025 showed how much our teams share in creativity and craftsmanship, and that trust led us to our unique spin on the iconic Clifton. The editorial tone they delivered to this project reflects the perfect sensation of everyday and extraordinary at once.”

#6816. Research: Lack of Specialized Infrastructure Is a Top AI Adoption Challenge

Enterprise AI projects are running into infrastructure questions as organizations move from planning toward deployment, according to recent research Digital Realty. Forty percent of survey respondents cited a lack of specialized infrastructure required by AI as a main challenge to adopting a formal AI strategy, up from 9% in the company's 2024 research.

The Global Data Insights Survey 2026 points to a widening mix of infrastructure models behind enterprise AI. Half of respondents host AI models in private clouds, 86% are pursuing or exploring sovereign AI, 92% tie data-location strategy to their AI plans, and 88% follow a distributed-data approach.

The research is based on an online survey of 2,131 IT decision-makers across 19 countries, and 11 industries, conducted from Nov. 23, 2025, through Feb. 4, 2026.

Organizations also appear to be shifting from AI planning toward execution. The share actively executing AI to achieve operational efficiency or reduce costs rose from 27% in 2024 to 37% this year. Only 3% reported no measurable ROI to date, while 63% expect returns within the next six months to two years.

Specialized infrastructure led the survey's list of AI adoption challenges at 40%, followed by regulatory and personal-data concerns at 35%. Data availability and quality, storage capacity, interconnection, compute resources, and staffing also ranked as significant constraints.

Those requirements are also shaping workload placement. Integration with existing IT or data infrastructure ranked first at 43%, while regulatory compliance and performance were each cited by 37%. Data proximity, sovereignty, sustainability, cost, and intellectual property protection also factored into hosting decisions.

Private cloud features prominently across the report's AI hosting categories, but enterprises are clearly using a mix of private cloud, public cloud, on-premises infrastructure, and colocation. Private cloud accounted for 50% of custom-built AI model hosting and 47% for open source or fine-tuned large language models (LLMs).

Sovereignty is another part of that placement decision. Thirty-two percent consider sovereign AI — in which data and AI models remain within a single national or regulatory jurisdiction — a requirement, while another 54% are exploring it. The findings point toward hybrid environments in which infrastructure location, governance and data control increasingly overlap.

At a fundamental level, screen time policies and laws aim to address academic achievement by balancing digital instruction with traditional teaching methods. But they do not tackle how devices are used.

As schools become more dependent on digital platforms for instruction, grading, student services, and family communication, resilience depends not only on preventing attacks, but on how quickly leaders can restore trusted data, recover essential services, and keep disruptions from cascading across the school community.

Governance of the Open Secure AI Alliance has moved to the Linux Foundation, giving the initiative what the organizations describe as a neutral home for developing open source tools, shared standards and defensive practices. The Alliance was launched by NVIDIA in July to develop open security technologies for AI systems and agents.

The federal Education Freedom Tax Credit (EFTC) is a new source of funding for education that could benefit 90% of students in states that have opted in to the program. However, some confusion and misconceptions exist surrounding details of the program, including which programs may qualify, how parents can apply for scholarships, and how public school students can benefit.

#6811. OpenAI Intros Dots, AI Agents that Continue Working Between User Conversations

OpenAI has introduced Dots, GPT-6 Astra-powered agents that run on their own cloud computers, using connected applications to carry on with assignments between conversations and incorporating users' feedback as they go.

OpenAI is also previewing specialist Dots for organizations, with separate identities and credentials for defined responsibilities. Those enterprise deployments are beginning with focused pilots. The company is working with Microsoft on integration with Agent 365's governance and security controls.

The broader ambition seems to be to make delegation last beyond a chat session. That change could reduce the need to repeatedly explain a project. It also makes supervision a continuing obligation: An instruction that was appropriate yesterday may no longer fit today's circumstances.

Users currently cannot inspect or directly edit individual Dot memories. Deleting a Dot deletes its own context, while files and conversations it created remain separately stored. Plugin permissions are shared across Dots, ChatGPT, ChatGPT Work, and Codex.

For an organization, that creates two related governance questions: what an agent can retrieve now, and what it already knows. Revoking a connection addresses the first without necessarily resolving the second.

OpenAI's safety account distinguishes authorized tasks that continue in the background from unsolicited "proactive research." The latter uses tools restricted to reading permitted sources. Those research tasks cannot directly send messages, change connected application content, or control a browser or desktop. Any subsequent action must pass the usual rules and checks.

A separate system, Auto-review, checks certain planned actions against instructions and safety requirements. OpenAI says the agent cannot modify the controls enforcing those checks.

"Dots can still make mistakes," the company cautions.

OpenAI's Dots system-card appendix acknowledges that persistence and multi-agent systems are not new. Its evaluations examine how continuing work affects authorization boundaries.

In one test involving successive related tasks, moderate scope violations increased from 8.6% to 19.7% when the number of intervening tasks doubled from five to 10. Examples included carrying information between unrelated tasks or editing a shared document beyond the intended scope. The company reported no severe breaches or exfiltration in that evaluation.

These are controlled test results, not measured failure rates in customer deployments. They nevertheless identify a problem that a successful demonstration cannot settle: whether an agent keeps the right boundaries as its workload accumulates.

According to OpenAI's DevDay recap, Dots are available to Pro and Business Premium users in eligible markets. Enterprise, Edu, and Healthcare access is an administrator-enabled beta, off by default.

The operational question is how organizations will keep delegated authority current. A continuing assignment needs a human owner who can revise its scope and decide when to stop it. Otherwise, the convenience of leaving work with an agent can outlast the reason it was authorized.

For more information, go to the OpenAI site.

Hide stains, protect upholstery, and add instant polish.

Just think about it: a dated, yellowing couch suddenly looks fresh and brand new. You can even go bold and experiment with patterned options, without the commitment of buying a whole new piece. Pro tip: patterns are also a great way to hide stains.
